I’ll start with the trucks and somehow I’m on Thursdays or the church grill or my boss’s tone, and I can see people not following me.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but it has been happening enough at work that I figured I should ask about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you use any alcohol or drugs?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think there’s anything significant to say there. I mean, I’m not trying to dodge the question—I just want to answer it the right way. Are you asking about recreational drugs, or alcohol like daily drinking? Because nothing stands out to me as a big issue, but I can be more specific if that helps.\n\nDoctor: Do you drink alcohol daily?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. Not daily. I might have a drink here or there, but it’s not something I’d describe as an everyday thing. I hope that answers it clearly.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever hear or see things that other people don't?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way people mean, like hearing voices or seeing things standing there. I mean, sometimes I might think I heard someone say my name or a noise in the store, but I figure everybody gets that once in a while, right? Nothing I’d want to make a big deal out of unless you think there’s something specific I should be looking for.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience this disjointed speech when you're talking to friends or family, outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, maybe a little. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but my family has noticed I can kind of lose the thread when I’m trying to explain something.\n\nIt’s more obvious at work, like when I’m talking about inventory numbers or the weekend schedule. I’ll start with the truck delivery, then somehow I’m on Thursday, then I mention the church grill thing, then my boss’s tone, and people just sort of look at me like they’re not following. With friends or family, it can happen too, but I think they’re more patient with me. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your disjointed speech is related to your alcohol use?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s really from drinking, no. I mean, I don’t drink every day, so it doesn’t seem to line up that way to me.\n\nThe reason I came in was more because when I’m trying to explain things at work, like inventory numbers or the weekend schedule, my words start kind of jumping around—from the truck delivery to Thursday to the church grill thing, then something about my boss’s tone—and people look at me like they can’t follow what I’m saying. I’m not trying to be confusing. It just comes out that way sometimes. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your difficulty explaining things is affecting your relationships with friends, family, or your job?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess it has, more than I’d like to admit.\n\nAt work they’re not really letting me run the busy weekend shifts anymore, which… I mean, I understand they need things to go smoothly, but it doesn’t feel good. And after my last performance review, my boss said if things don’t improve I could be suspended. I’m trying not to overstate it, but that scared me.\n\nAt home, my wife has had to take over some things I used to handle, like commitments with other people, because I guess people were getting uncomfortable or confused when I was the one talking through things. I don’t mean to make people uneasy. It’s just… I think it’s starting to affect more than I wanted to admit.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like your thoughts are racing or hard to control when you're trying to explain something?\n\nPatient: Yeah… maybe not “racing” exactly, or at least I don’t know if that’s the right word. It’s more like when I’m trying to explain something, it stops staying in a straight line.\n\nLike with the inventory numbers or the weekend schedule, I’ll mean to talk about one thing, and then my words jump from the truck delivery to Thursday, then to the church grill thing, then something about my boss’s tone. And I can see people looking at me like they can’t follow it.\n\nI’m not trying to be all over the place. It just kind of comes out that way sometimes. Is that what you mean?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your disjointed speech is related to your alcohol use?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s really from drinking, no. I mean, I don’t drink every day, so it doesn’t seem to line up that way to me.\n\nThe reason I came in was more because when I’m trying to explain things at work, like inventory numbers or the weekend schedule, my words start kind of jumping around—from the truck delivery to Thursday to the church grill thing, then something about my boss’s tone—and people look at me like they can’t follow what I’m saying. I’m not trying to be confusing. It just comes out that way sometimes. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your difficulty explaining things is affecting your relationships with friends, family, or your job?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess it has, more than I’d like to admit.\n\nAt work they’re not really letting me run the busy weekend shifts anymore, which… I mean, I understand they need things to go smoothly, but it doesn’t feel good. And after my last performance review, my boss said if things don’t improve I could be suspended. I’m trying not to overstate it, but that scared me.\n\nAt home, my wife has had to take over some things I used to handle, like commitments with other people, because I guess people were getting uncomfortable or confused when I was the one talking through things. I don’t mean to make people uneasy. It’s just… I think it’s starting to affect more than I wanted to admit.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like your thoughts are racing or hard to control when you're trying to explain something?\n\nPatient: Yeah… maybe not “racing” exactly, or at least I don’t know if that’s the right word. It’s more like when I’m trying to explain something, it stops staying in a straight line.\n\nLike with the inventory numbers or the weekend schedule, I’ll mean to talk about one thing, and then my words jump from the truck delivery to Thursday, then to the church grill thing, then something about my boss’s tone. And I can see people looking at me like they can’t follow it.\n\nI’m not trying to be all over the place. It just kind of comes out that way sometimes. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you notice your speech becoming more disjointed when you're feeling anxious, stressed, or upset?\n\nPatient: I guess… maybe it can be a little worse when I’m stressed, but I don’t want to blame it all on that.\n\nThe main thing I’ve noticed is just when I’m trying to explain something clearly—like the inventory numbers or the weekend schedule—my words start jumping around. I’ll go from the truck delivery to Thursday, then somehow to the church grill, then something about my boss’s tone, and people look at me like they can’t follow me.\n\nI’m not trying to make it sound worse than it is. It’s just… that’s kind of why I came in, because it keeps happening. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you notice any difference in your speech when you have not had a drink for a while?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I haven’t really noticed a clear difference tied to that.\n\nI mean, I don’t want to answer wrong—if you mean like after not drinking for a few hours or a day or something, I can try to pay attention to it. I don’t know that I can say it improves after a week without drinking, exactly. I haven’t tracked it that way, and I don’t want to give you a wrong answer.

I’ll start with the trucks and somehow I’m on Thursdays or the church grill or my boss’s tone, and I can see people not following me.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but it has been happening enough at work that I figured I should ask about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you use any alcohol or drugs?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think there’s anything significant to say there. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but my family has noticed I can kind of lose the thread when I’m trying to explain something.\n\nIt’s more obvious at work, like when I’m talking about inventory numbers or the weekend schedule. I’ll start with the truck delivery, then somehow I’m on Thursday, then I mention the church grill thing, then my boss’s tone, and people just sort of look at me like they’re not following. Like I’ll be talking about trucks, then Thursdays, then something about the church grill, then my boss’s tone, and people just look at me like they can’t follow me.\n\nI don’t mean to make it confusing. I’m trying to be clear. I just thought maybe I should get it checked, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you have these episodes of disjointed speech all the time, or do they come and go?\n\nPatient: It’s not every single second, no. I mean, I can talk normally a lot of the time, I think. It’s more when I’m trying to explain something with details, like inventory numbers or the weekend schedule, then it sort of… slips. I’ll start with the trucks and somehow I’m on Thursdays or the church grill or my boss’s tone, and I can see people not following me.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but it has been happening enough at work that I figured I should ask about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you use any alcohol or drugs?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think there’s anything significant to say there. I mean, I’m not trying to dodge the question—I just want to answer it the right way. Are you asking about recreational drugs, or alcohol like daily drinking? Because nothing stands out to me as a big issue, but I can be more specific if that helps.\n\nDoctor: Do you drink alcohol daily?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. Not daily. I might have a drink here or there, but it’s not something I’d describe as an everyday thing. I hope that answers it clearly.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever hear or see things that other people don't?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way people mean, like hearing voices or seeing things standing there. I mean, sometimes I might think I heard someone say my name or a noise in the store, but I figure everybody gets that once in a while, right? Nothing I’d want to make a big deal out of unless you think there’s something specific I should be looking for.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience this disjointed speech when you're talking to friends or family, outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, maybe a little. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but my family has noticed I can kind of lose the thread when I’m trying to explain something.\n\nIt’s more obvious at work, like when I’m talking about inventory numbers or the weekend schedule. I’ll start with the truck delivery, then somehow I’m on Thursday, then I mention the church grill thing, then my boss’s tone, and people just sort of look at me like they’re not following. With friends or family, it can happen too, but I think they’re more patient with me. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your disjointed speech is related to your alcohol use?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s really from drinking, no. I mean, I don’t drink every day, so it doesn’t seem to line up that way to me.\n\nThe reason I came in was more because when I’m trying to explain things at work, like inventory numbers or the weekend schedule, my words start kind of jumping around—from the truck delivery to Thursday to the church grill thing, then something about my boss’s tone—and people look at me like they can’t follow what I’m saying. I’m not trying to be confusing. It just comes out that way sometimes. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your difficulty explaining things is affecting your relationships with friends, family, or your job?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess it has, more than I’d like to admit.\n\nAt work they’re not really letting me run the busy weekend shifts anymore, which… I mean, I understand they need things to go smoothly, but it doesn’t feel good. And after my last performance review, my boss said if things don’t improve I could be suspended. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you notice your speech becoming more disjointed when you're feeling anxious, stressed, or upset?\n\nPatient: I guess… maybe it can be a little worse when I’m stressed, but I don’t want to blame it all on that.\n\nThe main thing I’ve noticed is just when I’m trying to explain something clearly—like the inventory numbers or the weekend schedule—my words start jumping around. Like I’ll be talking about trucks, then Thursdays, then something about the church grill, then my boss’s tone, and people just look at me like they can’t follow me.\n\nI don’t mean to make it confusing. I’m trying to be clear. I just thought maybe I should get it checked, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you have these episodes of disjointed speech all the time, or do they come and go?\n\nPatient: It’s not every single second, no. I mean, I can talk normally a lot of the time, I think. It’s more when I’m trying to explain something with details, like inventory numbers or the weekend schedule, then it sort of… slips. I’ll start with the trucks and somehow I’m on Thursdays or the church grill or my boss’s tone, and I can see people not following me.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but it has been happening enough at work that I figured I should ask about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you use any alcohol or drugs?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think there’s anything significant to say there. I mean, I’m not trying to dodge the question—I just want to answer it the right way. Are you asking about recreational drugs, or alcohol like daily drinking? Because nothing stands out to me as a big issue, but I can be more specific if that helps.\n\nDoctor: Do you drink alcohol daily?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. Not daily. I might have a drink here or there, but it’s not something I’d describe as an everyday thing. I hope that answers it clearly.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever hear or see things that other people don't?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way people mean, like hearing voices or seeing things standing there. I mean, sometimes I might think I heard someone say my name or a noise in the store, but I figure everybody gets that once in a while, right? Nothing I’d want to make a big deal out of unless you think there’s something specific I should be looking for.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience this disjointed speech when you're talking to friends or family, outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, maybe a little. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but my family has noticed I can kind of lose the thread when I’m trying to explain something.\n\nIt’s more obvious at work, like when I’m talking about inventory numbers or the weekend schedule. I’ll start with the truck delivery, then somehow I’m on Thursday, then I mention the church grill thing, then my boss’s tone, and people just sort of look at me like they’re not following. With friends or family, it can happen too, but I think they’re more patient with me. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your disjointed speech is related to your alcohol use?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s really from drinking, no. I mean, I don’t drink every day, so it doesn’t seem to line up that way to me.\n\nThe reason I came in was more because when I’m trying to explain things at work, like inventory numbers or the weekend schedule, my words start kind of jumping around—from the truck delivery to Thursday to the church grill thing, then something about my boss’s tone—and people look at me like they can’t follow what I’m saying. I’m not trying to be confusing. It just comes out that way sometimes. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your difficulty explaining things is affecting your relationships with friends, family, or your job?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess it has, more than I’d like to admit.\n\nAt work they’re not really letting me run the busy weekend shifts anymore, which… I mean, I understand they need things to go smoothly, but it doesn’t feel good. And after my last performance review, my boss said if things don’t improve I could be suspended. I’m trying not to overstate it, but that scared me.\n\nAt home, my wife has had to take over some things I used to handle, like commitments with other people, because I guess people were getting uncomfortable or confused when I was the one talking through things. I don’t mean to make people uneasy. It’s just… I think it’s starting to affect more than I wanted to admit.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like your thoughts are racing or hard to control when you're trying to explain something?\n\nPatient: Yeah… maybe not “racing” exactly, or at least I don’t know if that’s the right word. It’s more like when I’m trying to explain something, it stops staying in a straight line.\n\nLike with the inventory numbers or the weekend schedule, I’ll mean to talk about one thing, and then my words jump from the truck delivery to Thursday, then to the church grill thing, then something about my boss’s tone. And I can see people looking at me like they can’t follow it.\n\nI’m not trying to be all over the place. It just kind of comes out that way sometimes. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you notice your speech becoming more disjointed when you're feeling anxious, stressed, or upset?\n\nPatient: I guess… maybe it can be a little worse when I’m stressed, but I don’t want to blame it all on that.\n\nThe main thing I’ve noticed is just when I’m trying to explain something clearly—like the inventory numbers or the weekend schedule—my words start jumping around. I’ll go from the truck delivery to Thursday, then somehow to the church grill, then something about my boss’s tone, and people look at me like they can’t follow me.\n\nI’m not trying to make it sound worse than it is. It’s just… that’s kind of why I came in, because it keeps happening. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you notice any difference in your speech when you have not had a drink for a while?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I haven’t really noticed a clear difference tied to that.\n\nI mean, I don’t want to answer wrong—if you mean like after not drinking for a few hours or a day or something, I can try to pay attention to it. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess I’m here because work’s been a little harder to explain lately. I’m a supervisor in retail, and when I try to go over inventory numbers or the weekend schedule, I start out fine, but then my words kind of… jump around. Like I’ll be talking about trucks, then Thursdays, then something about the church grill, then my boss’s tone, and people just look at me like they can’t follow me.\n\nI don’t mean to make it confusing. I’m trying to be clear. I just thought maybe I should get it checked, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you have these episodes of disjointed speech all the time, or do they come and go?\n\nPatient: It’s not every single second, no. I mean, I can talk normally a lot of the time, I think. It’s more when I’m trying to explain something with details, like inventory numbers or the weekend schedule, then it sort of… slips. I’ll start with the trucks and somehow I’m on Thursdays or the church grill or my boss’s tone, and I can see people not following me.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but it has been happening enough at work that I figured I should ask about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you use any alcohol or drugs?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think there’s anything significant to say there. I mean, I’m not trying to dodge the question—I just want to answer it the right way. Are you asking about recreational drugs, or alcohol like daily drinking? Because nothing stands out to me as a big issue, but I can be more specific if that helps.\n\nDoctor: Do you drink alcohol daily?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. Not daily. I might have a drink here or there, but it’s not something I’d describe as an everyday thing. I hope that answers it clearly.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever hear or see things that other people don't?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way people mean, like hearing voices or seeing things standing there. I mean, sometimes I might think I heard someone say my name or a noise in the store, but I figure everybody gets that once in a while, right? Nothing I’d want to make a big deal out of unless you think there’s something specific I should be looking for.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience this disjointed speech when you're talking to friends or family, outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, maybe a little. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but my family has noticed I can kind of lose the thread when I’m trying to explain something.\n\nIt’s more obvious at work, like when I’m talking about inventory numbers or the weekend schedule. I’ll start with the truck delivery, then somehow I’m on Thursday, then I mention the church grill thing, then my boss’s tone, and people just sort of look at me like they’re not following. With friends or family, it can happen too, but I think they’re more patient with me. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your disjointed speech is related to your alcohol use?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s really from drinking, no. I mean, I don’t drink every day, so it doesn’t seem to line up that way to me.\n\nThe reason I came in was more because when I’m trying to explain things at work, like inventory numbers or the weekend schedule, my words start kind of jumping around—from the truck delivery to Thursday to the church grill thing, then something about my boss’s tone—and people look at me like they can’t follow what I’m saying. I’m not trying to be confusing. It just comes out that way sometimes. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your difficulty explaining things is affecting your relationships with friends, family, or your job?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess it has, more than I’d like to admit.\n\nAt work they’re not really letting me run the busy weekend shifts anymore, which… I mean, I understand they need things to go smoothly, but it doesn’t feel good. And after my last performance review, my boss said if things don’t improve I could be suspended. I’m trying not to overstate it, but that scared me.\n\nAt home, my wife has had to take over some things I used to handle, like commitments with other people, because I guess people were getting uncomfortable or confused when I was the one talking through things. I don’t mean to make people uneasy. It’s just… I think it’s starting to affect more than I wanted to admit.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like your thoughts are racing or hard to control when you're trying to explain something?\n\nPatient: Yeah… maybe not “racing” exactly, or at least I don’t know if that’s the right word. It’s more like when I’m trying to explain something, it stops staying in a straight line.\n\nLike with the inventory numbers or the weekend schedule, I’ll mean to talk about one thing, and then my words jump from the truck delivery to Thursday, then to the church grill thing, then something about my boss’s tone. And I can see people looking at me like they can’t follow it.\n\nI’m not trying to be all over the place. It just kind of comes out that way sometimes. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you notice your speech becoming more disjointed when you're feeling anxious, stressed, or upset?\n\nPatient: I guess… maybe it can be a little worse when I’m stressed, but I don’t want to blame it all on that.\n\nThe main thing I’ve noticed is just when I’m trying to explain something clearly—like the inventory numbers or the weekend schedule—my words start jumping around. I’ll go from the truck delivery to Thursday, then somehow to the church grill, then something about my boss’s tone, and people look at me like they can’t follow me.\n\nI’m not trying to make it sound worse than it is. It’s just… that’s kind of why I came in, because it keeps happening. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you notice any difference in your speech when you have not had a drink for a while?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I haven’t really noticed a clear difference tied to that.\n\nI mean, I don’t want to answer wrong—if you mean like after not drinking for a few hours or a day or something, I can try to pay attention to it. But so far, I’ve mostly noticed the problem when I’m trying to explain things, not specifically whether I’ve had a drink recently or not.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had this kind of disjointed speech, or is it something that has developed over time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s always been like this, no. I mean, I’ve always maybe gone into too much detail sometimes, but this kind of jumping around… that feels more recent.\n\nI’d say it’s been building over, maybe, the last 11 months or so. I don’t want to make it sound like it’s all because of him or anything, but that’s about when I started noticing people having a harder time following me. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any improvement in your speech when you haven't been drinking at all for an extended period, like a week or more?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can say it improves after a week without drinking, exactly. I haven’t tracked it that way, and I don’t want to give you a wrong answer.\n\nWhat I keep noticing is more that when I’m trying to explain something at work, like the inventory numbers or the weekend schedule, my words kind of jump around. Is that okay to say?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where your disjointed speech was accompanied by feelings of being detached from reality?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it detached from reality, exactly. I don’t want to overstate it.\n\nThere have been times where I’m talking and I sort of lose the thread, and afterward I think, “That probably didn’t come out right.” But feeling like I wasn’t in reality… I don’t know. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you notice any difference in your speech when you have not had a drink for a while?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I haven’t really noticed a clear difference tied to that.\n\nI mean, I don’t want to answer wrong—if you mean like after not drinking for a few hours or a day or something, I can try to pay attention to it. But so far, I’ve mostly noticed the problem when I’m trying to explain things, not specifically whether I’ve had a drink recently or not.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had this kind of disjointed speech, or is it something that has developed over time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s always been like this, no. I mean, I’ve always maybe gone into too much detail sometimes, but this kind of jumping around… that feels more recent.\n\nI’d say it’s been building over, maybe, the last 11 months or so. Around when the new district manager came in and started pushing harder on the forecasts and schedules. I don’t want to make it sound like it’s all because of him or anything, but that’s about when I started noticing people having a harder time following me. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any improvement in your speech when you haven't been drinking at all for an extended period, like a week or more?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can say it improves after a week without drinking, exactly. I haven’t tracked it that way, and I don’t want to give you a wrong answer.\n\nWhat I keep noticing is more that when I’m trying to explain something at work, like the inventory numbers or the weekend schedule, my words kind of jump around. I’ll start with the trucks, then I’m on Thursday, then somehow I’m talking about the church grill or my boss’s tone, and people look at me like they’re lost.\n\nSo… I guess I haven’t seen a clear “better because I wasn’t drinking” pattern. Is that okay to say?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where your disjointed speech was accompanied by feelings of being detached from reality?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it detached from reality, exactly. I don’t want to overstate it.\n\nThere have been times where I’m talking and I sort of lose the thread, and afterward I think, “That probably didn’t come out right.” But feeling like I wasn’t in reality… I don’t know. Maybe I’d need you to explain what you mean by that a little more.\n\nI don’t want to say yes if that’s not really what’s happening.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you were not in touch with what was real?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way that sounds, anyway.\n\nI mean, I’ve been confused or overwhelmed, sure, and sometimes I’ll look back at something I said and think, “Why did I explain it like that?” But actually not knowing what was real… I don’t know that I’ve had that.\n\nIf there’s a specific kind of example you mean, I can try to answer better. I just don’t want to say yes to something that isn’t quite right.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had times when you felt like your thoughts were not your own?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I mean, I’ve had thoughts I didn’t like, or thoughts that felt kind of scattered, but not like they belonged to somebody else.\n\nAt least, I don’t think that’s happened. I’m trying to answer it the right way. If you mean like someone putting thoughts in my head, I wouldn’t say that. Not that I’ve noticed.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but my family has noticed I can kind of lose the thread when I’m trying to explain something.\n\nIt’s more obvious at work, like when I’m talking about inventory numbers or the weekend schedule. I don’t mean to make people uneasy. It’s just… I think it’s starting to affect more than I wanted to admit.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like your thoughts are racing or hard to control when you're trying to explain something?\n\nPatient: Yeah… maybe not “racing” exactly, or at least I don’t know if that’s the right word. It’s more like when I’m trying to explain something, it stops staying in a straight line.\n\nLike with the inventory numbers or the weekend schedule, I’ll mean to talk about one thing, and then my words jump from the truck delivery to Thursday, then to the church grill thing, then something about my boss’s tone. And I can see people looking at me like they can’t follow it.\n\nI’m not trying to be all over the place. It just kind of comes out that way sometimes. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you notice your speech becoming more disjointed when you're feeling anxious, stressed, or upset?\n\nPatient: I guess… maybe it can be a little worse when I’m stressed, but I don’t want to blame it all on that.\n\nThe main thing I’ve noticed is just when I’m trying to explain something clearly—like the inventory numbers or the weekend schedule—my words start jumping around. I’ll go from the truck delivery to Thursday, then somehow to the church grill, then something about my boss’s tone, and people look at me like they can’t follow me.\n\nI’m not trying to make it sound worse than it is. It’s just… that’s kind of why I came in, because it keeps happening. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you notice any difference in your speech when you have not had a drink for a while?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I haven’t really noticed a clear difference tied to that.\n\nI mean, I don’t want to answer wrong—if you mean like after not drinking for a few hours or a day or something, I can try to pay attention to it. But so far, I’ve mostly noticed the problem when I’m trying to explain things, not specifically whether I’ve had a drink recently or not.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had this kind of disjointed speech, or is it something that has developed over time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s always been like this, no. I mean, I’ve always maybe gone into too much detail sometimes, but this kind of jumping around… that feels more recent.\n\nI’d say it’s been building over, maybe, the last 11 months or so. Around when the new district manager came in and started pushing harder on the forecasts and schedules. I don’t want to make it sound like it’s all because of him or anything, but that’s about when I started noticing people having a harder time following me. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any improvement in your speech when you haven't been drinking at all for an extended period, like a week or more?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can say it improves after a week without drinking, exactly. I haven’t tracked it that way, and I don’t want to give you a wrong answer.\n\nWhat I keep noticing is more that when I’m trying to explain something at work, like the inventory numbers or the weekend schedule, my words kind of jump around. I’ll start with the trucks, then I’m on Thursday, then somehow I’m talking about the church grill or my boss’s tone, and people look at me like they’re lost.\n\nSo… I guess I haven’t seen a clear “better because I wasn’t drinking” pattern. Is that okay to say?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where your disjointed speech was accompanied by feelings of being detached from reality?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it detached from reality, exactly. I don’t want to overstate it.\n\nThere have been times where I’m talking and I sort of lose the thread, and afterward I think, “That probably didn’t come out right.” But feeling like I wasn’t in reality… I don’t know. Maybe I’d need you to explain what you mean by that a little more.\n\nI don’t want to say yes if that’s not really what’s happening.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you were not in touch with what was real?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way that sounds, anyway.\n\nI mean, I’ve been confused or overwhelmed, sure, and sometimes I’ll look back at something I said and think, “Why did I explain it like that?” But actually not knowing what was real… I don’t know that I’ve had that.\n\nIf there’s a specific kind of example you mean, I can try to answer better. I just don’t want to say yes to something that isn’t quite right.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had times when you felt like your thoughts were not your own?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I mean, I’ve had thoughts I didn’t like, or thoughts that felt kind of scattered, but not like they belonged to somebody else.\n\nAt least, I don’t think that’s happened. I’m trying to answer it the right way. If you mean like someone putting thoughts in my head, I wouldn’t say that. Not that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your disjointed speech started after you began drinking alcohol?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d connect it to drinking, honestly. I mean, I’ve had alcohol at times like most people, but I haven’t noticed a clear “this started after that” kind of pattern.\n\nIf you think that’s something I should look at more closely, I can try to think it through, but offhand I wouldn’t want to blame it on drinking if I’m not sure.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I hope that answers it clearly.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever hear or see things that other people don't?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way people mean, like hearing voices or seeing things standing there. I mean, sometimes I might think I heard someone say my name or a noise in the store, but I figure everybody gets that once in a while, right? Nothing I’d want to make a big deal out of unless you think there’s something specific I should be looking for.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience this disjointed speech when you're talking to friends or family, outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, maybe a little. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but my family has noticed I can kind of lose the thread when I’m trying to explain something.\n\nIt’s more obvious at work, like when I’m talking about inventory numbers or the weekend schedule. I’ll start with the truck delivery, then somehow I’m on Thursday, then I mention the church grill thing, then my boss’s tone, and people just sort of look at me like they’re not following. With friends or family, it can happen too, but I think they’re more patient with me. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your disjointed speech is related to your alcohol use?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s really from drinking, no. I mean, I don’t drink every day, so it doesn’t seem to line up that way to me.\n\nThe reason I came in was more because when I’m trying to explain things at work, like inventory numbers or the weekend schedule, my words start kind of jumping around—from the truck delivery to Thursday to the church grill thing, then something about my boss’s tone—and people look at me like they can’t follow what I’m saying. I’m not trying to be confusing. It just comes out that way sometimes. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your difficulty explaining things is affecting your relationships with friends, family, or your job?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess it has, more than I’d like to admit.\n\nAt work they’re not really letting me run the busy weekend shifts anymore, which… I mean, I understand they need things to go smoothly, but it doesn’t feel good. And after my last performance review, my boss said if things don’t improve I could be suspended. I’m trying not to overstate it, but that scared me.\n\nAt home, my wife has had to take over some things I used to handle, like commitments with other people, because I guess people were getting uncomfortable or confused when I was the one talking through things. I don’t mean to make people uneasy. It’s just… I think it’s starting to affect more than I wanted to admit.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like your thoughts are racing or hard to control when you're trying to explain something?\n\nPatient: Yeah… maybe not “racing” exactly, or at least I don’t know if that’s the right word. It’s more like when I’m trying to explain something, it stops staying in a straight line.\n\nLike with the inventory numbers or the weekend schedule, I’ll mean to talk about one thing, and then my words jump from the truck delivery to Thursday, then to the church grill thing, then something about my boss’s tone. And I can see people looking at me like they can’t follow it.\n\nI’m not trying to be all over the place. It just kind of comes out that way sometimes. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you notice your speech becoming more disjointed when you're feeling anxious, stressed, or upset?\n\nPatient: I guess… maybe it can be a little worse when I’m stressed, but I don’t want to blame it all on that.\n\nThe main thing I’ve noticed is just when I’m trying to explain something clearly—like the inventory numbers or the weekend schedule—my words start jumping around. I’ll go from the truck delivery to Thursday, then somehow to the church grill, then something about my boss’s tone, and people look at me like they can’t follow me.\n\nI’m not trying to make it sound worse than it is. It’s just… that’s kind of why I came in, because it keeps happening. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you notice any difference in your speech when you have not had a drink for a while?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I haven’t really noticed a clear difference tied to that.\n\nI mean, I don’t want to answer wrong—if you mean like after not drinking for a few hours or a day or something, I can try to pay attention to it. But so far, I’ve mostly noticed the problem when I’m trying to explain things, not specifically whether I’ve had a drink recently or not.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had this kind of disjointed speech, or is it something that has developed over time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s always been like this, no. I mean, I’ve always maybe gone into too much detail sometimes, but this kind of jumping around… that feels more recent.\n\nI’d say it’s been building over, maybe, the last 11 months or so. Around when the new district manager came in and started pushing harder on the forecasts and schedules. I don’t want to make it sound like it’s all because of him or anything, but that’s about when I started noticing people having a harder time following me. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any improvement in your speech when you haven't been drinking at all for an extended period, like a week or more?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can say it improves after a week without drinking, exactly. I haven’t tracked it that way, and I don’t want to give you a wrong answer.\n\nWhat I keep noticing is more that when I’m trying to explain something at work, like the inventory numbers or the weekend schedule, my words kind of jump around. I’ll start with the trucks, then I’m on Thursday, then somehow I’m talking about the church grill or my boss’s tone, and people look at me like they’re lost.\n\nSo… I guess I haven’t seen a clear “better because I wasn’t drinking” pattern. Is that okay to say?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where your disjointed speech was accompanied by feelings of being detached from reality?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it detached from reality, exactly. I don’t want to overstate it.\n\nThere have been times where I’m talking and I sort of lose the thread, and afterward I think, “That probably didn’t come out right.” But feeling like I wasn’t in reality… I don’t know. Maybe I’d need you to explain what you mean by that a little more.\n\nI don’t want to say yes if that’s not really what’s happening.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you were not in touch with what was real?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way that sounds, anyway.\n\nI mean, I’ve been confused or overwhelmed, sure, and sometimes I’ll look back at something I said and think, “Why did I explain it like that?” But actually not knowing what was real… I don’t know that I’ve had that.\n\nIf there’s a specific kind of example you mean, I can try to answer better. I just don’t want to say yes to something that isn’t quite right.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had times when you felt like your thoughts were not your own?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I mean, I’ve had thoughts I didn’t like, or thoughts that felt kind of scattered, but not like they belonged to somebody else.\n\nAt least, I don’t think that’s happened. I’m trying to answer it the right way. If you mean like someone putting thoughts in my head, I wouldn’t say that. Not that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your disjointed speech started after you began drinking alcohol?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d connect it to drinking, honestly. I mean, I’ve had alcohol at times like most people, but I haven’t noticed a clear “this started after that” kind of pattern.\n\nIf you think that’s something I should look at more closely, I can try to think it through, but offhand I wouldn’t want to blame it on drinking if I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where your disjointed speech was accompanied by hallucinations or delusions?\n\nPatient: Not that I can clearly point to, no.\n\nI’ve had times where I wasn’t explaining myself well, or my words came out kind of tangled, but I don’t think there was anything like seeing or hearing things, or being convinced of something strange at the same time. At least not in a way I’d know to call that.\n\nI hope that answers what you mean.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. It’s more like when I’m trying to explain something, it stops staying in a straight line.\n\nLike with the inventory numbers or the weekend schedule, I’ll mean to talk about one thing, and then my words jump from the truck delivery to Thursday, then to the church grill thing, then something about my boss’s tone. And I can see people looking at me like they can’t follow it.\n\nI’m not trying to be all over the place. It just kind of comes out that way sometimes. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you notice your speech becoming more disjointed when you're feeling anxious, stressed, or upset?\n\nPatient: I guess… maybe it can be a little worse when I’m stressed, but I don’t want to blame it all on that.\n\nThe main thing I’ve noticed is just when I’m trying to explain something clearly—like the inventory numbers or the weekend schedule—my words start jumping around. I’ll go from the truck delivery to Thursday, then somehow to the church grill, then something about my boss’s tone, and people look at me like they can’t follow me.\n\nI’m not trying to make it sound worse than it is. It’s just… that’s kind of why I came in, because it keeps happening. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you notice any difference in your speech when you have not had a drink for a while?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I haven’t really noticed a clear difference tied to that.\n\nI mean, I don’t want to answer wrong—if you mean like after not drinking for a few hours or a day or something, I can try to pay attention to it. But so far, I’ve mostly noticed the problem when I’m trying to explain things, not specifically whether I’ve had a drink recently or not.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had this kind of disjointed speech, or is it something that has developed over time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s always been like this, no. I mean, I’ve always maybe gone into too much detail sometimes, but this kind of jumping around… that feels more recent.\n\nI’d say it’s been building over, maybe, the last 11 months or so. Around when the new district manager came in and started pushing harder on the forecasts and schedules. I don’t want to make it sound like it’s all because of him or anything, but that’s about when I started noticing people having a harder time following me. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any improvement in your speech when you haven't been drinking at all for an extended period, like a week or more?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can say it improves after a week without drinking, exactly. I haven’t tracked it that way, and I don’t want to give you a wrong answer.\n\nWhat I keep noticing is more that when I’m trying to explain something at work, like the inventory numbers or the weekend schedule, my words kind of jump around. I’ll start with the trucks, then I’m on Thursday, then somehow I’m talking about the church grill or my boss’s tone, and people look at me like they’re lost.\n\nSo… I guess I haven’t seen a clear “better because I wasn’t drinking” pattern. Is that okay to say?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where your disjointed speech was accompanied by feelings of being detached from reality?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it detached from reality, exactly. I don’t want to overstate it.\n\nThere have been times where I’m talking and I sort of lose the thread, and afterward I think, “That probably didn’t come out right.” But feeling like I wasn’t in reality… I don’t know. Maybe I’d need you to explain what you mean by that a little more.\n\nI don’t want to say yes if that’s not really what’s happening.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you were not in touch with what was real?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way that sounds, anyway.\n\nI mean, I’ve been confused or overwhelmed, sure, and sometimes I’ll look back at something I said and think, “Why did I explain it like that?” But actually not knowing what was real… I don’t know that I’ve had that.\n\nIf there’s a specific kind of example you mean, I can try to answer better. I just don’t want to say yes to something that isn’t quite right.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had times when you felt like your thoughts were not your own?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I mean, I’ve had thoughts I didn’t like, or thoughts that felt kind of scattered, but not like they belonged to somebody else.\n\nAt least, I don’t think that’s happened. I’m trying to answer it the right way. If you mean like someone putting thoughts in my head, I wouldn’t say that. Not that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your disjointed speech started after you began drinking alcohol?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d connect it to drinking, honestly. I mean, I’ve had alcohol at times like most people, but I haven’t noticed a clear “this started after that” kind of pattern.\n\nIf you think that’s something I should look at more closely, I can try to think it through, but offhand I wouldn’t want to blame it on drinking if I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where your disjointed speech was accompanied by hallucinations or delusions?\n\nPatient: Not that I can clearly point to, no.\n\nI’ve had times where I wasn’t explaining myself well, or my words came out kind of tangled, but I don’t think there was anything like seeing or hearing things, or being convinced of something strange at the same time. At least not in a way I’d know to call that.\n\nI hope that answers what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Is your disjointed speech something that happens all the time, or are there times when it doesn't happen at all?\n\nPatient: It’s not all the time, no. I can have normal conversations, I think. Like if it’s simple or we’re just talking casually, I don’t think people have trouble following me.\n\nIt’s more when I’m trying to explain something with a lot of pieces, like inventory numbers or the weekend schedule at work. Then I start… I don’t know, jumping around. I’ll be talking about the truck delivery, then suddenly Thursdays, then something about the church grill, then my boss’s tone, and I can see people looking at me like I’m not making sense.\n\nI don’t mean to do it. I’m trying to be clear. It just sort of gets tangled sometimes. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your speech would improve if you completely stopped drinking alcohol?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, doctor. I mean, I wouldn’t want to say no if you think it could be connected, but I haven’t noticed a clear difference like my speech gets better when I’m not drinking.\n\nIf it would help, I could try stopping and pay closer attention. I just don’t know that I’ve seen that pattern myself.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
